The following proposition is borrowed from Lemma 3 in
69
.

Proposition G.5
.

Suppose
\gamma=\ell_{\gamma}
.
Denote
\overline{D}:=(n\lambda+1)\cdot n^{-1}\Sigma_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}^{-1}+n^{-1}\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}^{\top}\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}
,
then we have

\lambda_{\text{max}}\left(\overline{D}\right)=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)\quad\text{ and }\quad\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(\overline{D}\right)=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1).

Recall that
\tilde{\ell}=\min\{\ell_{\gamma},\ell_{\lambda}\}
. Hence, from Assumption 4 we have

\lambda d^{\ell_{\lambda}}=O_{d}(1),\text{ and }\lambda d^{\ell_{\lambda}+1}\rightarrow\infty.

(77)

In the proof of the next lemma, we will frequently use (
77
).

Lemma G.6
.

We have the following bounds:

(i)

\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(M\right)=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda+1)
,

\mathrm{tr}(M/n)=O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda+1)
.

(ii)

M_{>\ell_{\gamma}}=K_{>\ell_{\gamma}}+n\lambda\mathrm{I}_{n}=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda+1)\left(\mathrm{I}_{n}+\Delta_{1}\right)
.

(iii)

When
\ell_{\lambda}+1\leq\ell_{\gamma}
, for any
i=N_{\ell_{\lambda}}+1,\cdots,\lfloor\min\{n,N_{\ell_{\lambda}+1}\}\rfloor
,
we have

\lambda_{i}(M)=O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda+1).

(iv)

When
\ell_{\lambda}<\ell_{\gamma}
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\text{max}}(M_{>\ell_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda),\quad\lambda_{\text{min}}(M_{>\ell_{\lambda}})=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda),

and
\lambda_{\text{max}}(K_{>\ell_{\lambda}})=O_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left({d^{\gamma-\ell_{\lambda}-1}}\right)=o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda)
.

(v)

Denote
A=n^{-1}\Sigma_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}+n^{-1}\Psi_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{\top}M_{>\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}\Psi_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}
, then we have

\lambda_{\text{max}}\left(A^{-1}\right)=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda+1)\quad\text{ and }\quad\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(A^{-1}\right)=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda+1).

Proof.

(i)
From Lemma
G.1
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(M\right)\overset{\text{Proposition }\ref{prop_weyl_ine}}{\geq}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}\Sigma_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}^{\top}\right)+\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(\kappa_{1}\mathrm{I}_{n}\right)+\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(\kappa_{1}\Delta_{1}\right)+n\lambda

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}(1-o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1))+n\lambda=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda+1).

Further, from Lemma
G.1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left(\frac{M}{n}\right)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left(\frac{K}{n}+\lambda\mathrm{I}_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left(\frac{1}{n}\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}\Sigma_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}^{\top}\right)+\mathrm{tr}\left(\frac{\kappa_{1}}{n}\mathrm{I}_{n}\right)+\mathrm{tr}\left(\frac{\kappa_{1}}{n}\mathbf{\Delta}_{1}\right)+n\lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left(\frac{1}{n}\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}^{\top}\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}\Sigma_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}\right)+\kappa_{1}\left(1+o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)\right)+n\lambda

\displaystyle\overset{\text{Lemma }\ref{lemma psi top psi}\text{ and Lemma }\ref{lemma psi top psi_int}}{=}

\displaystyle O_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(\mathrm{tr}\left(\Sigma_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}\right)\right)+\kappa_{1}\left(1+o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)\right)+n\lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda+1),

(ii)
It is a direct corollary of Lemma
G.1
.

(iii)

Denote the SVD decomposition of
\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}
as
\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}=n^{1/2}~OHV^{\top}
, where
O\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and
V\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{\ell_{\gamma}}\times N_{\ell_{\gamma}}}
are orthogonal matrices,
H=\left[H_{\star};0\right]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times N_{\ell_{\gamma}}}
, and
H_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{\ell_{\gamma}}\times N_{\ell_{\gamma}}}
is a diagonal matrix. Then, Lemma
G.2
and Lemma
G.3
imply that both
\lambda_{\text{max}}\left(H_{\star}\right)
and
\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(H_{\star}\right)
are of order
\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)
.

Recall the assumption that
\ell_{\lambda}+1\leq\ell_{\gamma}
. Hence, for any
i=N_{\ell_{\lambda}}+1,\cdots,\lfloor\min\{n,N_{\ell_{\lambda}+1}\}\rfloor
, from Lemma
G.1
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\left(M\right)=

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\left(\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}\Sigma_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}^{\top}+M_{>\ell_{\gamma}}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{Proposition~ \ref{prop_weyl_ine}}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\left(\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}\Sigma_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}^{\top}\right)+\lambda_{1}\left(M_{>\ell_{\gamma}}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{Part (ii)}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\left(\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}\Sigma_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}^{\top}\right)+O_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(n\lambda+1\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\left(H_{\star}V^{\top}\Sigma_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}VH_{\star}^{\top}\right)+O_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(n\lambda+1\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lambda_{\text{max}}\left(H_{\star}\right)^{2}\lambda_{i}\left(\Sigma_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}\right)+O_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(n\lambda+1\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\mu_{\ell_{\lambda}+1}+n\lambda+1)\overset{(\ref{eqn:regular_para_upper})}{=}O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda+1).

(iv)
When
\ell_{\lambda}<\ell_{\gamma}
, we have
1=o_{d}(n\lambda)
and
nd^{-\ell_{\lambda}-1}=\Omega_{d}(1)
.
As in Case (i), we can show that
\lambda_{\text{min}}(M_{>\ell_{\lambda}})=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda+1)=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda)
. On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\text{max}}(M_{>\ell_{\lambda}})\overset{\text{Proposition }\ref{prop_weyl_ine}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\text{max}}(K_{\ell_{\lambda}+1,\ell_{\gamma}})+\lambda_{\text{max}}(K_{>\ell_{\gamma}})+n\lambda

(78)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{Lemma }\ref{lemma psi psi top}}{=}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\text{max}}(K_{\ell_{\lambda}+1,\ell_{\gamma}})+O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda)

Denote the SVD decomposition of
\Psi_{\ell_{\lambda}+1,\ell_{\gamma}}
as
\Psi_{\ell_{\lambda}+1,\ell_{\gamma}}=n^{1/2}~OHV^{\top}
, where
O\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and
V\in\mathbb{R}^{(N_{\ell_{\gamma}}-N_{\ell_{\lambda}})\times(N_{\ell_{\gamma}}-N_{\ell_{\lambda}})}
are orthogonal matrices;
H=\left[H_{\star};0\right]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times(N_{\ell_{\gamma}}-N_{\ell_{\lambda}})}
and
H_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{(N_{\ell_{\gamma}}-N_{\ell_{\lambda}})\times(N_{\ell_{\gamma}}-N_{\ell_{\lambda}})}
is a diagonal matrix.
Then, Lemma
G.2
and Lemma
G.3
imply that both
\lambda_{\text{max}}\left(H_{\star}\right)
and
\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(H_{\star}\right)
are of order
\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\text{max}}(K_{\ell_{\lambda}+1,\ell_{\gamma}})=

\displaystyle\lambda_{\text{max}}\left(\Psi_{\ell_{\lambda}+1,\ell_{\gamma}}\Sigma_{\ell_{\lambda}+1,\ell_{\gamma}}\Psi_{\ell_{\lambda}+1,\ell_{\gamma}}^{\top}\right)

(79)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{n}{d^{\ell_{\lambda}+1}}\right)\overset{(\ref{eqn:regular_para_upper})}{=}o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda).

Combining all these, we have that both
\lambda_{\text{max}}(M_{>\ell_{\lambda}})
and
\lambda_{\text{min}}(M_{>\ell_{\lambda}})
are of order
\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda)
.
Finally, as in (
78
) and (
79
), we can show that
\lambda_{\text{max}}(K_{>\ell_{\lambda}})=O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(nd^{-\ell_{\lambda}-1}+1)=O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(nd^{-\ell_{\lambda}-1})=o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n\lambda)
, finishing the proof of (iv).

(v)
We have
A=n^{-1}\Sigma_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}+n^{-1}\Psi_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{\top}M_{>\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}\Psi_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}
. On the one hand, from Lemma
G.2
, Lemma
G.3
, (ii), and (iv), we have

\lambda_{\text{max}}\left(A\right)=O_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{d^{\tilde{\ell}}}{n}+\frac{1}{n(\lambda+n^{-1})}\right)\overset{(\ref{eqn:regular_para_upper})}{=}O_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{1}{n\lambda+1}\right);

On the other hand, to obtain the lower bound of
\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(A\right)
, we consider the following three cases:
(a)
\gamma>\ell_{\gamma}
;
(b)
\gamma=\ell_{\gamma}
and
\ell_{\lambda}<\ell_{\gamma}
;
(b)
\gamma=\ell_{\gamma}
and
\ell_{\lambda}\geq\ell_{\gamma}
.

Case (a).

When
\gamma>\ell_{\gamma}
, from Lemma
G.2
, (ii), and (iv), we have

\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(A\right)\overset{\text{Proposition }\ref{prop_weyl_ine}}{\geq}\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(n^{-1}\Psi_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{\top}M_{>\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}\Psi_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right)=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{1}{n\lambda+1}\right).

Case (b).

When
\gamma=\ell_{\gamma}
and
\tilde{\ell}=\ell_{\lambda}<\ell_{\gamma}
,
from Lemma
G.3
and (iv), we have

\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(A\right)\geq\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(n^{-1}\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\lambda}}^{\top}M_{>\ell_{\lambda}}^{-1}\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\lambda}}\right)=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{1}{n\lambda+1}\right).

Case (c).
When
\gamma=\ell_{\gamma}
and
\ell_{\lambda}\geq\ell_{\gamma}
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(A\right)\overset{\text{(ii)}}{\geq}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(n^{-1}\Sigma_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}^{-1}+(n\lambda+1)^{-1}n^{-1}\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}^{\top}\Psi_{\leq\ell_{\gamma}}\right)(1-o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1))

\displaystyle\overset{\text{Proposition }\ref{prop_lemma_3_in_xiao}}{=}

\displaystyle\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{1}{n\lambda+1}\right),

and we get the desired results.

\blacksquare

With some modifications, the following lemma restates Lemma 8, 9, and 10 in
77
.

Lemma G.7
.

We have

(i)

\left\|\Psi_{>\tilde{\ell}}\theta_{>\tilde{\ell}}\right\|_{2}^{2}=O_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(n\|\theta_{>\tilde{\ell}}\|_{2}^{2}\right)
.

(ii)

\|\theta_{>\tilde{\ell}}\|_{2}^{2}=\Theta_{d}\left(d^{-(\tilde{\ell}+1)s}\right)
.

(iii)

\left\|\Sigma_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{-\tau^{\prime}}\theta_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\Theta_{d}\left(d^{(2\tau^{\prime}-\min\{s,2\tau^{\prime}\})\tilde{\ell}}\right)
,
\tau^{\prime}\geq 1
.

Proof.

(i) and (ii) are direct results of Lemma S8 and S10 in
77
. Now let’s prove (iii). We have

\left\|\Sigma_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{-\tau^{\prime}}\theta_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\tilde{\ell}}\mu_{m}^{-2\tau^{\prime}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N(d,m)}(\theta_{m,j})^{2}=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\tilde{\ell}}\mu_{m}^{s-2\tau^{\prime}}\mu_{m}^{-s}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N(d,m)}(\theta_{m,j})^{2}.

(80)

On the one hand, when
0\leq s\leq 2\tau^{\prime}
, (
80
) implies

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{-\tau^{\prime}}\theta_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{Assumption }3}{=}O_{d}\left(\mu_{\tilde{\ell}}^{s-2\tau^{\prime}}\right)=O_{d}\left(d^{(2\tau^{\prime}-s)\tilde{\ell}}\right)

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{-\tau^{\prime}}\theta_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\mu_{\tilde{\ell}}^{s-2\tau^{\prime}}\mu_{\tilde{\ell}}^{s}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N(d,\tilde{\ell})}(\theta_{\tilde{\ell},j})^{2}\overset{\text{Assumption }6}{=}\Omega_{d}\left(d^{(2\tau^{\prime}-s)\tilde{\ell}}\right).

On the other hand, when
s>2\tau^{\prime}
,
since
\mu_{m}=O_{d}(1)
,
m=0,1,\cdots,\tilde{\ell}
, (
80
) implies

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{-\tau^{\prime}}\theta_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{Assumption }3}{=}O_{d}\left(1\right)

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{-\tau^{\prime}}\theta_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{d}(1)\cdot\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\tilde{\ell}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N(d,m)}(\theta_{m,j})^{2}\overset{\text{Assumption }6}{=}\Omega_{d}(1),

and we obtain the desired results.

\blacksquare

The following two identities are based on the Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula (see, e.g., Proposition
K.4
).

Lemma G.8
.

For
k\in\{\ell_{\gamma},\ell_{\lambda}\}
, we have

\displaystyle M^{-1}\Psi_{\leq k}\Sigma_{\leq k}^{1/2}=

\displaystyle M_{>k}^{-1}\Psi_{\leq k}\Sigma_{\leq k}^{1/2}\left(\mathrm{I}_{N_{k}}+\Sigma_{\leq k}^{1/2}\Psi_{\leq k}^{\top}M_{>k}^{-1}\Psi_{\leq k}\Sigma_{\leq k}^{1/2}\right)^{-1},

and

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\leq k}-\Sigma_{\leq k}\Psi^{\top}_{\leq k}M^{-1}\Psi_{\leq k}\Sigma_{\leq k}=

\displaystyle\left(\Sigma_{\leq k}^{-1}+\Psi_{\leq k}^{\top}M_{>k}^{-1}\Psi_{\leq k}\right)^{-1}.

Proof.

Recall that we have
M=\Psi_{\leq k}\Sigma_{\leq k}\Psi_{\leq k}^{-}{\top}+M_{>k}
.

•

Applying the Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula with
Z=\Psi_{\leq k}\Sigma_{\leq k}^{1/2}
and
A=M_{>k}
, we have

\displaystyle M^{-1}\Psi_{\leq k}\Sigma_{\leq k}^{1/2}=

\displaystyle(ZZ^{\top}+A)^{-1}Z=A^{-1}Z-A^{-1}Z(\mathrm{I}_{N_{k}}+Z^{\top}A^{-1}Z)^{-1}Z^{\top}A^{-1}Z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A^{-1}Z\left(\mathrm{I}_{N_{k}}-(\mathrm{I}_{N_{k}}+Z^{\top}A^{-1}Z)^{-1}\left(\mathrm{I}_{N_{k}}+Z^{\top}A^{-1}Z-\mathrm{I}_{N_{k}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A^{-1}Z\left(\mathrm{I}_{N_{k}}+Z^{\top}A^{-1}Z\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M_{>k}^{-1}\Psi_{\leq k}\Sigma_{\leq k}^{1/2}\left(\mathrm{I}_{N_{k}}+\Sigma_{\leq k}^{1/2}\Psi_{\leq k}^{\top}M_{>k}^{-1}\Psi_{\leq k}\Sigma_{\leq k}^{1/2}\right)^{-1}.

•

Applying the Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula with
Z=\Psi_{\leq k}M_{>k}^{-1/2}
and
A=\Sigma_{\leq k}^{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\Sigma_{\leq k}^{-1}+\Psi_{\leq k}^{\top}M_{>k}^{-1}\Psi_{\leq k}\right)^{-1}=

\displaystyle(ZZ^{\top}+A)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A^{-1}-A^{-1}Z(\mathrm{I}_{B_{k}}+Z^{\top}A^{-1}Z)^{-1}Z^{\top}A^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\leq k}-\Sigma_{\leq k}\Psi^{\top}_{\leq k}M^{-1}\Psi_{\leq k}\Sigma_{\leq k}.

\blacksquare

Appendix H
Proof of results
in Section 4

In this section, we will generalize the results for spherical inner-product kernels defined on the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d}
to kernels on general domains and prove Theorem 4.2.

Throughout this section, we assume that all conditions and assumptions in Theorem 4.2 hold.

We use the same notations as in Appendix
A
, except that each symbol is augmented with a tilde, e.g.,
\Psi\to\tilde{\Psi}
and
\Sigma\to\tilde{\Sigma}
.

H.1
Kernel approximation results for general kernels

The following lemma is a corollary of Theorem 6 in
54
, which generalizes the results in Lemma
G.1
and Lemma
G.2
.

Lemma H.1
.

We have

\displaystyle\tilde{\Psi}_{>\ell_{\gamma}}\tilde{\Sigma}_{>\ell_{\gamma}}\tilde{\Psi}_{>\ell_{\gamma}}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{1}\left(\mathrm{I}_{n}+\Delta_{1}\right),\quad\|\Delta_{1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1),

(81)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{\Psi}_{>\ell_{\gamma}}\tilde{\Sigma}_{>\ell_{\gamma}}^{2}\tilde{\Psi}_{>\ell_{\gamma}}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{2}\left(\mathrm{I}_{n}+\Delta_{2}\right),\quad\|\Delta_{2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1);

(82)

with

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}=\Theta_{d}(1),\quad\kappa_{2}=\Theta_{d}\left(d^{-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)}\right);

Moreover, denote
\tilde{N}_{\ell_{\gamma}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell_{\gamma}}\tilde{N}(d,k)
, then we have

\left\|\frac{\tilde{\Psi}_{\leq\ell}^{\top}\tilde{\Psi}_{\leq\ell}}{n}-\mathrm{I}_{\tilde{N}_{\ell_{\gamma}}}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1).

(83)

Proof.

While Theorem 6 in
54
is stated under the setting of random feature kernels, its proofs (mainly relying on Propositions 3 and 4 in
54
) do not rely on any property specific to this class. Consequently, the results remain valid for general kernels, and we adopt them in this broader setting.

Proof of (
81
) and (
83
).

We first state the following conditions (i.e., Assumption 6 (a), Assumption 6 (b), and Assumption 7 (a) in
54
with the following substitution of notations:
u(d)\to\sum_{k=0}^{2\ell_{\gamma}+1}\tilde{N}(d,k)
,
N(d)\to n
,
M(d)\to\sum_{k=0}^{\ell_{\gamma}}\tilde{N}(d,k)
, and
\{\lambda_{d,j}^{2}\}\to\{\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}\}
):

•

For any integer
q\geq 1
, there exists
C=C(q,\ell)
, such that for any
k\leq 2\ell+1
and any
j\leq\tilde{N}(d,k)
, we have

\|\tilde{\psi}_{k,j}\|_{L^{2q}}\leq C(q,\ell)\|\tilde{\psi}_{k,j}\|_{L^{2}}.

(84)

•

There exists
\delta_{1}>0
, such that

n^{2+\delta_{1}}\leq\frac{\left[\sum_{k\geq 2\ell_{\gamma}+2}\sum_{j\leq\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}\right]^{2}}{\sum_{k\geq 2\ell_{\gamma}+2}\sum_{j\leq\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}^{2}}.

(85)

•

There exists
\delta_{2}>0
, such that

n^{1+\delta_{2}}\leq\frac{\sum_{k\geq\ell_{\gamma}+1}\sum_{j\leq\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}}{\tilde{\lambda}_{\ell_{\gamma}+1,1}}.

(86)

Now let’s verify these conditions. Since
\|\tilde{\psi}_{k,j}\|_{L^{2}}=1
, Assumption 9 implies (
84
).
Assumption 8 implies that
\sum_{j\leq\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}=\Theta(1)
for any
k\leq 2\ell_{\gamma}+2
.
Together with Assumption 7, it further implies that there exist positive constants
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\left[\sum_{k\geq 2\ell_{\gamma}+2}\sum_{j\leq\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}\right]^{2}}{\sum_{k\geq 2\ell_{\gamma}+2}\sum_{j\leq\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}^{2}}\geq\frac{\left[\sum_{j\leq\tilde{N}(d,2\ell_{\gamma}+2)}\tilde{\lambda}_{2\ell_{\gamma}+2,j}\right]^{2}}{\tilde{\lambda}_{2\ell_{\gamma}+2,1}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j\leq\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}}=\Omega_{d}(d^{2(\ell_{\gamma}+1)})\gg n^{2+\delta_{1}},

and

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{k\geq\ell_{\gamma}+1}\sum_{j\leq\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}}{\tilde{\lambda}_{\ell_{\gamma}+1,1}}\geq\frac{\sum_{j\leq\tilde{N}(d,\ell_{\gamma}+1)}\tilde{\lambda}_{\ell_{\gamma}+1,j}}{\tilde{\lambda}_{\ell_{\gamma}+1,1}}=\Omega_{d}(d^{\ell_{\gamma}+1})\gg n^{1+\delta_{2}}.

Therefore, Theorem 6 in
54
ensures that both (
81
) and (
83
) hold.

Proof of (
82
).

Assumption 7 and 8 imply that there exist
\delta_{3},\delta_{4}>0
, such that

\displaystyle\frac{\left[\sum_{k\geq 2\ell_{\gamma}+2}\sum_{j\leq\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}^{2}\right]^{2}}{\sum_{k\geq 2\ell_{\gamma}+2}\sum_{j\leq\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}^{4}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{2\ell_{\gamma}+2,\tilde{N}(d,2\ell_{\gamma}+2)}^{2}\left[\sum_{j\leq\tilde{N}(d,2\ell_{\gamma}+2)}\tilde{\lambda}_{2\ell_{\gamma}+2,j}\right]^{2}}{\tilde{\lambda}_{2\ell_{\gamma}+2,1}^{3}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j\leq\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{d}(d^{2(\ell_{\gamma}+1)})

\displaystyle\gg n^{2+\delta_{3}},

and

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{k\geq\ell_{\gamma}+1}\sum_{j\leq\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}^{2}}{\tilde{\lambda}_{\ell_{\gamma}+1,1}^{2}}\geq\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{\ell_{\gamma}+1,\tilde{N}(d,\ell_{\gamma}+1)}\sum_{j\leq\tilde{N}(d,\ell_{\gamma}+1)}\tilde{\lambda}_{\ell_{\gamma}+1,j}}{\tilde{\lambda}_{\ell_{\gamma}+1,1}^{2}}=\Omega_{d}(d^{\ell_{\gamma}+1})\gg n^{1+\delta_{4}}.

Similar as above, from Theorem 6 in
54
with the substitution

\tilde{\mathscr{K}}(x,x^{\prime}):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}\tilde{\psi}_{k,j}(x)\tilde{\psi}_{k,j}\left(x^{\prime}\right)\to\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}^{2}\tilde{\psi}_{k,j}(x)\tilde{\psi}_{k,j}\left(x^{\prime}\right),

we have that (
82
) holds.

\blacksquare

The next lemma generalizes the results in Lemma
G.7
. We can obtain its proof by modified the corresponding proof for Lemma S8 and S10 in
77
, and Lemma
G.7
, and hence we omit the proof.

Lemma H.2
.

We have

(i)

\left\|\tilde{\Psi}_{>\tilde{\ell}}\theta_{>\tilde{\ell}}\right\|_{2}^{2}=O_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(n\|\theta_{>\tilde{\ell}}\|_{2}^{2}\right)
.

(ii)

\|\theta_{>\tilde{\ell}}\|_{2}^{2}=\Theta_{d}\left(d^{-(\tilde{\ell}+1)s}\right)
.

(iii)

\left\|\tilde{\Sigma}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}\theta_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\Theta_{d}\left(d^{(2-\min\{s,2\})\tilde{\ell}}\right)
.

H.2
Proof of Theorem 4.2

Proof.

Adopt the notations in (
67
). We divide the proof into four steps.

Step (I).
We first show that results in Appendix
G
can be generalized to the cases where kernels are defined on general domains.

Recall that
\gamma>\ell_{\gamma}
. We have:

•

Lemma
G.1
and Lemma
G.2
can be replaced by Lemma
H.1
.

•

Since the proof of Lemma
G.6
relies on Lemma
G.1
and Lemma
G.2
, it can be established in a similar manner by replacing them with Lemma
H.1
.

•

Lemma
G.7
can be replaced by Lemma
H.2
.

•

Lemma
G.8
still holds with each symbol augmented with a tilde.

Step (II).
We next show that the results for the variance term in Appendix
D
can also be extended to the case where kernels are defined on general domains.

Since the proof of Theorem
D.1
depends on the results established in Appendix
G
, Step (I) directly implies that

\displaystyle\mathrm{var}\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}\right)=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{v_{1}}+d^{v_{2}}\right).

Step (III).
We then bound the bias term by modifying the proof in Appendix
E
.

From (
50
) we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{bias}^{2}(\hat{f}_{\lambda})=

\displaystyle\|B_{1}+B_{2}\|_{2}^{2}+\|B_{3}-B_{4}-B_{5}\|_{2}^{2}.

•

In the proof of Theorem
E.1
, since results for
B_{2},B_{3},B_{4}
and
B_{5}
depend on the results established in Appendix
G
, Step (I) directly implies that (see also (
68
)):

\displaystyle\|B_{2}\|_{2}^{2}=

\displaystyle O_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{b_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle\|B_{3}-B_{5}\|_{2}^{2}=

\displaystyle\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{b_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle\|B_{4}\|_{2}^{2}=

\displaystyle o_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{b_{2}(u,1)}+d^{b_{3}}\right).

•

We then bound the term
\|B_{1}\|_{2}^{2}
. Notice that for kernel ridge regression, we have

\varphi_{\lambda,\tilde{K}}:=n^{-1}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\mathtt{KRR}}(\tilde{K}/n)=(\tilde{K}+n\lambda\mathrm{I}_{n})^{-1}=\tilde{M}^{-1}.

(87)

Moreover, from Step (I), the generalization of Lemma
G.8
and Lemma
G.6
(v) imply that

\displaystyle\tilde{\Sigma}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}-\tilde{\Sigma}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\tilde{\Psi}^{\top}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\tilde{M}^{-1}\tilde{\Psi}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\tilde{\Sigma}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}=\left(\tilde{\Sigma}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}+\tilde{\Psi}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{\top}\tilde{M}_{>\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}\tilde{\Psi}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right)^{-1},

(88)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\text{max}}\left(\left(\tilde{\Sigma}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}+\tilde{\Psi}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{\top}\tilde{M}_{>\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}\tilde{\Psi}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right)^{-1}\right)=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{n}\right),

(89)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\text{min}}\left(\left(\tilde{\Sigma}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}+\tilde{\Psi}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{\top}\tilde{M}_{>\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}\tilde{\Psi}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right)^{-1}\right)=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{n}\right).

(90)

Therefore, for
B_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\|B_{1}\|_{2}^{2}=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\mathrm{I}_{\tilde{N}_{\tilde{\ell}}}-\tilde{\Sigma}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\tilde{\Psi}^{\top}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\varphi_{\lambda,\tilde{K}}\tilde{\Psi}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right)\theta_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle(\because(\ref{eqn_inter_general_1}))\quad{=}

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\tilde{\Sigma}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}-\tilde{\Sigma}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\tilde{\Psi}^{\top}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\tilde{M}^{-1}\tilde{\Psi}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\tilde{\Sigma}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right)\tilde{\Sigma}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}\theta_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle(\because(\ref{eqn_inter_general_2}))\quad{=}

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\tilde{\Sigma}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}+\tilde{\Psi}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{\top}\tilde{M}_{>\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}\tilde{\Psi}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right)^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}\theta_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle(\because(\ref{eqn_inter_general_3})\text{ and }(\ref{eqn_inter_general_4}))\quad{=}

\displaystyle\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}(d^{-2u}+d^{-2\gamma})\left\|\tilde{\Sigma}_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}^{-1}\theta_{\leq\tilde{\ell}}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle(\because\text{ Lemma \ref{lemma ge l l2_general}})\quad{=}

\displaystyle\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left((d^{-2u}+d^{-2\gamma})d^{(2-\min\{s,2\})\tilde{\ell}}\right)

\displaystyle{=}

\displaystyle\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{b_{2}(u,1)}+d^{b_{3}}\right).

Step (IV).
Finally we combine the estimates from Step (II) (variance) and Step (III) (bias decomposition) to obtain the learning curve.

From Lemma
F.5
we have
b_{3}\leq v_{2}
. Therefore:

•

Upper bound:

\displaystyle~E_{x,\epsilon}\left[\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}(x)-f_{\star}(x)\right)^{2}\right]\leq\mathrm{var}\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}\right)+\|B_{1}\|_{2}^{2}+\|B_{2}\|_{2}^{2}+\|B_{3}-B_{5}\|_{2}^{2}+\|B_{4}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~O_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{v_{1}}+d^{v_{2}}\right)+O_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{b_{1}}+d^{b_{2}(u,1)}+d^{b_{3}}\right)=O_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{v_{1}}+d^{v_{2}}+d^{b_{1}}+d^{b_{2}(u,1)}\right).

•

For lower bound, we analyze three possible cases:

–

Suppose
b_{3}<b_{2}(u,1)
and
b_{1}<b_{2}(u,1)
. Then

\displaystyle~E_{x,\epsilon}\left[\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}(x)-f_{\star}(x)\right)^{2}\right]\geq\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{v_{1}}+d^{v_{2}}\right)+\|B_{1}\|_{2}^{2}-\|B_{2}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{v_{1}}+d^{v_{2}}+d^{b_{2}(u,1)}\right)=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{v_{1}}+d^{v_{2}}+d^{b_{1}}+d^{b_{2}(u,1)}\right).

–

Suppose
b_{3}<b_{2}(u,1)
and
b_{1}\geq b_{2}(u,1)
. Then

\displaystyle~E_{x,\epsilon}\left[\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}(x)-f_{\star}(x)\right)^{2}\right]\geq\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{v_{1}}+d^{v_{2}}\right)+\|B_{3}-B_{5}\|_{2}^{2}-\|B_{4}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{v_{1}}+d^{v_{2}}+d^{b_{1}}\right)=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{v_{1}}+d^{v_{2}}+d^{b_{1}}+d^{b_{2}(u,1)}\right).

–

Suppose
b_{3}\geq b_{2}(u,1)
. Then we further have
\max\{b_{2}(u,1),b_{3}\}\leq v_{2}
, and hence

\displaystyle~E_{x,\epsilon}\left[\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}(x)-f_{\star}(x)\right)^{2}\right]\geq\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{v_{1}}+d^{v_{2}}\right)+\|B_{3}-B_{5}\|_{2}^{2}-\|B_{4}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{v_{1}}+d^{v_{2}}+d^{b_{1}}\right)=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{v_{1}}+d^{v_{2}}+d^{b_{1}}+d^{b_{2}(u,1)}\right).

In sum, we have

\displaystyle E_{x,\epsilon}\left[\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}(x)-f_{\star}(x)\right)^{2}\right]=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{v_{1}}+d^{v_{2}}+d^{b_{1}}+d^{b_{2}(u,1)}\right),

and we obtain the desired results.

\blacksquare

Appendix I
Proof of Other Results in the Main Text

I.1
Proof of Theorem 3.3

Proof.

Proof of (i).
Suppose
0\leq s\leq\Gamma(\gamma)
.
Recall that we have
p:=\lfloor\gamma/(s+1)\rfloor\leq\ell_{\gamma}:=\lfloor\gamma\rfloor
.
The proof is split into the following three steps:

Step I.
We first consider the case where
\gamma>0
is an integer. Then (16) and
0\leq s\leq\Gamma(\gamma)
imply that
s=0
.
Corollary
F.1
implies that

E_{x,\epsilon}\left[\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}(x)-f_{\star}(x)\right)^{2}\right]=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{p-\gamma}+d^{-(p+1)s}\right),

where the last equation follows from

p\leq\gamma
and
s=0
.

Step II.

We then consider the case where
\gamma>0
is not an integer and show
p=\ell_{\gamma}
under the following three cases:

•

When
\gamma\leq 0.5
, we have
p=\ell_{\gamma}=0
;

•

When
\gamma\in(\ell_{\gamma},\ell_{\gamma}+0.5]
for
\ell_{\gamma}=1,2,\cdots
, we have

\ell_{\gamma}\geq p=\lfloor\gamma/(s+1)\rfloor\geq\lfloor\gamma/(\Gamma(\gamma)+1)\rfloor=\lfloor\ell_{\gamma}\rfloor=\ell_{\gamma};

•

When
\gamma\in(\ell_{\gamma}+0.5,\ell_{\gamma}+1)
, we have

\ell_{\gamma}\geq p\geq\lfloor\gamma/(\Gamma(\gamma)+1)\rfloor=\left\lfloor\frac{\gamma(\ell_{\gamma}+1)}{2(\ell_{\gamma}+1)-\gamma}\right\rfloor\geq\left\lfloor\frac{(\ell_{\gamma}+0.5)(\ell_{\gamma}+1)}{2(\ell_{\gamma}+1)-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)}\right\rfloor=\ell_{\gamma}.

Furthermore, Proposition
F.4
implies that
p\leq u^{\prime}(\tau)<\gamma
. Therefore, for any
u\in[u^{\prime}(\tau),\infty]
, we have

\tilde{\ell}:=\min\{\ell_{\gamma},\lfloor u\rfloor\}=\min\{\ell_{\gamma},p\}=\ell_{\gamma}=p.

(91)

Step III.
Now we are ready to determine the convergence rate of the excess risk when
\gamma>0
is not an integer.
We first recall the definitions of certain notations, e.g.,
b_{i}=b_{i}(u)
and
v_{i}=v_{i}(u)
for
i=1,2
, in Appendix
F
.

From Theorem 3.1, we have

\displaystyle E_{x,\epsilon}\left[\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}(x)-f_{\star}(x)\right)^{2}\right]=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{v_{1}}+d^{v_{2}}+d^{b_{1}}+\mathbf{1}\{\tau<\infty\}d^{b_{2}(u,\tau)}\right);

Furthermore, Proposition
F.6
implies that, for any
u\in[u^{\prime}(\tau),\infty]
, we have

\displaystyle E_{x,\epsilon}\left[\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}(x)-f_{\star}(x)\right)^{2}\right]=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{v_{1}}+d^{v_{2}}+d^{b_{1}}\right).

(92)

We divide the remaining proof in two parts:

•

When
u\geq\gamma
, we have

\displaystyle v_{1}=\gamma-\ell_{\gamma}-1,

\displaystyle v_{2}=\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma,

\displaystyle b_{1}=-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)s.

Therefore, (
92
) implies

\displaystyle E_{x,\epsilon}\left[\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}(x)-f_{\star}(x)\right)^{2}\right]=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma}+d^{\gamma-\ell_{\gamma}-1}+d^{-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)s}\right).

(93)

Notice that the above rate is the same as the rate given in (13) of
77
. Following the same discussion under Corollary 1 in
77
, we have

\displaystyle E_{x,\epsilon}\left[\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}(x)-f_{\star}(x)\right)^{2}\right]=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma}+d^{\gamma-\ell_{\gamma}-1}+d^{-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)s}\right)=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{p-\gamma}+d^{-(p+1)s}\right).

(94)

(For completeness, we provide an alternative proof of (
94
) in Remark
I.1
.)

•

When
u^{\prime}(\tau)\leq u<\gamma
, from (
91
) we have

\displaystyle v_{1}=-\gamma-p-1+2u,

\displaystyle v_{2}=p-\gamma,

\displaystyle b_{1}=-(p+1)s.

–

When
\gamma\in(\ell_{\gamma},\ell_{\gamma}+0.5]
, from (
91
) we have
u<\ell_{\gamma}+0.5=p+0.5
, hence

v_{2}>v_{1}.

–

When
\gamma\in(\ell_{\gamma}+0.5,\ell_{\gamma}+1)
, we have

b_{1}-v_{1}=(p+1)(1-s)+\gamma-2u\overset{(16)}{\geq}2(\gamma-u)>0.

Therefore, for any
u\in[u^{\prime}(\tau),\infty]
, from (
92
) we have

\displaystyle E_{x,\epsilon}\left[\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}(x)-f_{\star}(x)\right)^{2}\right]=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{v_{2}}+d^{b_{1}}\right)=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{p-\gamma}+d^{-(p+1)s}\right).

Proof of (ii).
Suppose
s>\Gamma(\gamma)
.
When
u=\gamma
, we have
\tilde{\ell}=\ell_{\gamma}
, hence Theorem 3.1 implies

\displaystyle E_{x,\epsilon}\left[\left(\hat{f}_{d^{\gamma}}(x)-f_{\star}(x)\right)^{2}\right]=

\displaystyle\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma}+d^{\gamma-\ell_{\gamma}-1}+d^{-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)s}\right).

Notice that the above rate is the same as the one given in (13) of
77
. Therefore, following the same discussion under Corollary 1 in
77
, we have

\displaystyle E_{x,\epsilon}\left[\left(\hat{f}_{d^{\gamma}}(x)-f_{\star}(x)\right)^{2}\right]=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma}+d^{\gamma-\ell_{\gamma}-1}+d^{-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)s}\right)\gg d^{p-\gamma}+d^{-(p+1)s}\quad\text{ in prob.}

(95)

(For completeness, we provide an alternative proof of (
95
) in Remark
I.2
.)

Recall that Proposition
F.4
implies
\gamma>u^{\prime}
.
Notice that, for any non-integer
u
, Theorem 3.1 implies that the convergence rate of the excess risk
E_{x,\epsilon}\left[\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}(x)-f_{\star}(x)\right)^{2}\right]
is continuous with respect to
u
, hence there exists
\tilde{u}\in(u^{\prime},\gamma)
, such that for
\tilde{\lambda}=d^{-\tilde{u}}
, we have

\displaystyle E_{x,\epsilon}\left[\left(\hat{f}_{\tilde{\lambda}}(x)-f_{\star}(x)\right)^{2}\right]\gg d^{p-\gamma}+d^{-(p+1)s}\quad\text{ in prob.}

\blacksquare

Remark I.1
.

Here we provide an alternative proof for (
94
).
Notice that

\displaystyle\gamma-\ell_{\gamma}-1\left\{\begin{matrix}\leq[1-(\gamma-\ell_{\gamma})]-1=\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma&\text{ if }\gamma\in(\ell_{\gamma},\ell_{\gamma}+0.5]~\text{ and }~\ell_{\gamma}\geq 0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt=-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)\Gamma(\gamma)\leq-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)s&\text{ if }\gamma\in(\ell_{\gamma}+0.5,\ell_{\gamma}+1),\end{matrix}\right.

where the first case follows from
0<\gamma-\ell_{\gamma}\leq 0.5
and the second case follows from the definition of
\Gamma(\cdot)
and the assumption that
s\geq\Gamma(\gamma)
.
Recall that in
Step II
we have shown that
p=\ell_{\gamma}
, therefore we have

\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma}+d^{\gamma-\ell_{\gamma}-1}+d^{-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)s}\right)=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma}+d^{-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)s}\right)=\Theta_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{p-\gamma}+d^{-(p+1)s}\right).

Remark I.2
.

Here we provide an alternative proof for (
95
). We consider four different cases given by (16).

Case 1:
\gamma\in(0,0.5]
.
In this case we have
\Gamma(\gamma)=\infty
, hence it is impossible to let
s>\Gamma(\gamma)
.

Case 2:
\gamma\in(\ell_{\gamma},\ell_{\gamma}+0.5]~\text{ and }~\ell_{\gamma}\geq 1
.
In this case we have
\gamma/(s+1)<\gamma/(\Gamma(\gamma)+1)=\ell_{\gamma}
, and hence
p<\ell_{\gamma}
. Therefore, we have

\left\{\begin{aligned} \ell_{\gamma}-\gamma&\geq\gamma-\ell_{\gamma}-1,&&\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma&=-\ell_{\gamma}\Gamma(\gamma)>-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)\Gamma(\gamma)>-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)s,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma&>p-\gamma,\end{aligned}\right.

where the first inequality follows from
0<\gamma-\ell_{\gamma}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
.
Therefore, we have

\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{\gamma-\ell_{\gamma}-1}+d^{\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma}+d^{-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)s}\right)=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma}\right).

Furthermore, we have

\left\{\begin{aligned} -(p+1)s&<-\ell_{\gamma}\Gamma(\gamma)=\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma,&&\text{if }p=\ell_{\gamma}-1,\\
-(p+1)s&<p+1-\gamma<\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma,&&\text{if }p<\ell_{\gamma}-1,\end{aligned}\right.

where the first case uses
s>\Gamma(\gamma)
and (16), and the second case uses
\gamma<(p+1)(s+1)
by the definition of
p
.

Together with
p<\ell_{\gamma}
, we have

\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma}\right)\gg d^{p-\gamma}+d^{-(p+1)s}\quad\text{ in prob.}

Case 3:
\gamma\in(\ell_{\gamma}+0.5,\ell_{\gamma}+1)
.
In this case we have

\left\{\begin{aligned} \gamma-\ell_{\gamma}-1&>\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma,\qquad(\text{since }\gamma-\ell_{\gamma}>0.5)\\[4.0pt]
\gamma-\ell_{\gamma}-1&=-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)\Gamma(\gamma)>-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)s.\end{aligned}\right.

On one hand, when
p=\ell_{\gamma}
, we have

\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{\gamma-\ell_{\gamma}-1}+d^{\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma}+d^{-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)s}\right)\gg d^{\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma}+d^{-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)s}=d^{p-\gamma}+d^{-(p+1)s}\quad\text{ in prob.}

On the other hand, when
p<\ell_{\gamma}
, we have

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma>p-\gamma,\\
\vskip 3.0pt&\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma\geq p+1-\gamma>-(p+1)s,\end{aligned}\right.

where the last inequality follows from
\gamma<(p+1)(s+1)
.
Therefore, we have

\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{\gamma-\ell_{\gamma}-1}+d^{\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma}+d^{-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)s}\right)\gg d^{\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma}\gg d^{p-\gamma}+d^{-(p+1)s}\quad\text{ in prob.}

Case 4:
\gamma=1,2,\cdots
.
In this case we have
s>\Gamma(\gamma)=0
. Since
\gamma/(s+1)<\gamma=\ell_{\gamma}
, we further have
p<\ell_{\gamma}
. Therefore,
d^{\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma}
is of order
\Theta_{d}(1)
while
d^{p-\gamma}+d^{-(p+1)s}
is of order
o_{d}(1)
. Hence we have

\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(d^{\gamma-\ell_{\gamma}-1}+d^{\ell_{\gamma}-\gamma}+d^{-(\ell_{\gamma}+1)s}\right)=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)\gg d^{p-\gamma}+d^{-(p+1)s}\quad\text{ in prob.}

I.2
Estimator of sequence model in (17)

Proposition I.3
.

Consider the sequence model in (17).
Suppose
\lambda_{j}
’s are known and consider the parametrization
f_{j}=\lambda_{j}^{1/2}\beta_{j}
,
j=1,2,\cdots
.
Then, applying ridge regression with regularization parameter
\lambda
or gradient flow with early-stopping time
t=\lambda^{-1}
yields estimators of the form

\hat{f}_{j}^{\lambda}=\lambda_{j}\varphi_{\lambda}(\lambda_{j})z_{j},\quad j=1,2,\cdots,

where
\varphi_{\lambda}
is the regularization function
\varphi_{\lambda}^{\mathtt{KRR}}
or
\varphi_{\lambda}^{\mathtt{GF}}
defined in Section 2.3.1.

Proof.

Ridge regression.
Considering the loss
L(\hat{\beta})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(z_{j}-\lambda_{j}^{1/2}\hat{\beta}_{j})^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\hat{\beta}_{j}^{2}
. Minimizing the above loss with respect to each
\hat{\beta}_{j}
yields the closed-form solution
\hat{\beta}_{j}=\lambda_{j}^{1/2}(\lambda_{j}+\lambda)^{-1}z_{j}=\lambda_{j}^{1/2}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\mathtt{KRR}}(\lambda_{j})z_{j}
. Consequently, the estimator for
\theta_{j}
is given by:

\hat{f}_{j}^{\lambda}=\lambda_{j}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\mathtt{KRR}}(\lambda_{j})z_{j}.

Gradient flow.

Considering the loss
L(\hat{\beta})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(z_{j}-\lambda_{j}^{1/2}\hat{\beta}_{j})^{2}
.
The gradient flow dynamics, initialized at
\hat{\beta}_{j}(0)=0
, are governed by the differential equation:

\frac{\mathsf{d}}{\mathsf{d}t}\hat{\beta}_{j}(t)=-\frac{\partial L}{\partial\hat{\beta}_{j}}=\lambda_{j}^{1/2}(z_{j}-\lambda_{j}^{1/2}\hat{\beta}_{j}(t)),

and the solution to this equation is
\hat{\beta}_{j}=\lambda_{j}^{-1/2}(1+e^{-\lambda_{j}t})z_{j}=\lambda_{j}^{1/2}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\mathtt{GF}}(\lambda_{j})z_{j}
. Therefore, the corresponding estimator for
\theta_{j}
at time
t=\lambda^{-1}
is:

\hat{f}_{j}^{\lambda}=\lambda_{j}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\mathtt{GF}}(\lambda_{j})z_{j},

and we obtain the desired results.

\blacksquare

I.3
Proof of Proposition 3.4

Proof.

Denote

\mathcal{N}_{2,\varphi}(\lambda)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left[\lambda_{j}\varphi_{\lambda}(\lambda_{j})\right]^{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{M}_{2,\varphi}(\lambda)=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(\psi_{\lambda}(\lambda_{j})f_{j}\right)^{2}.

Then, when
\lambda>0
, Lemma D.14 in
49
implies that

\mathcal{N}_{2,\varphi}(\lambda)=\Theta_{d}\left(d^{2u-\ell_{\lambda}-1}+d^{\ell_{\lambda}}\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{M}_{2,\varphi}(\lambda)=\Theta_{d}\left(d^{-(\ell_{\lambda}+1)s}+\mathbf{1}\{\tau<\infty\}d^{-2\tau u+(2\tau-\tilde{s})\ell_{\lambda}}\right).

Therefore, (19) implies

E_{\xi}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(\hat{f}_{j}^{\lambda}-f_{j})^{2}\right]=\Theta_{d}\left(d^{2u-\gamma-\ell_{\lambda}-1}+d^{\ell_{\lambda}-\gamma}+d^{-(\ell_{\lambda}+1)s}+\mathbf{1}\{\tau<\infty\}d^{-2\tau u+(2\tau-\tilde{s})\ell_{\lambda}}\right).

\blacksquare

I.4
Examples of Assumption 8

In this subsection, we first define the Product Laguerre kernels. Then, we will verify that Assumption 8 holds for the kernels mentioned below it.

I.4.1
Definition of Product Laguerre kernels on Gamma measures

In this subsection, we will define a type of kernels based on Laguerre polynomials and Gamma measures, and show that it satisfies Assumption 8.

Fix positive constants
\{\alpha_{i}>0\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
and
r>0
, such that we have that
\sup_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}<\infty
.
For dimensions
d\geq 2r
, let
\mathcal{X}=[0,\infty)^{d}
be the positive orthant of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and let
\rho_{\mathcal{X}}
be the product measure where each coordinate
x_{(i)}
follows an independent Gamma distribution
\Gamma(\alpha_{i}+1,1)
,
i\leq d
.
Denote
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{d})^{\top}
.

For any
c>0
, let
I_{c}(z):=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}[m!\Gamma(m+c+1)]^{-1}(z/2)^{2m+c}
be the modified Bessel function of the first kind. Define

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}_{i}(z,z^{\prime})=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{i}+1)}{\left(1-r/d\right)}\left(\frac{rzz^{\prime}}{d}\right)^{-\alpha_{i}/2}\exp\left\{-\frac{\frac{r}{d}(z+z^{\prime})}{1-r/d}\right\}I_{\alpha_{i}}\left(\frac{2\sqrt{\frac{r}{d}zz^{\prime}}}{1-r/d}\right),\quad z,z^{\prime}\in[0,\infty),\quad i\leq d.

Finally, let’s define a mapping from
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
to
\mathbb{R}
:

\tilde{\mathscr{K}}_{\alpha,r}(x,x^{\prime}):=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\mathscr{K}_{i}(x_{(i)},x_{(i)}^{\prime}),\quad x,x^{\prime}\in[0,\infty)^{d}.

(96)

I.4.2
Verifying Assumption 8

In this part, we will show that Assumption 8 holds for several types of common kernels in large dimensions, that is, for these kernels, there exists an absolute constant
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}
such that
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}\leq\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}
; moreover, for any
k=0,1,\cdots,2\ell_{\gamma}+2
, we assume that

\tilde{N}(d,k)=\Theta_{d}(d^{k})\quad\text{ and }\quad\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}=\Theta_{d}(d^{-k}),\quad\text{ for }1\leq j\leq\tilde{N}(d,k).

(97)

Inner product kernels defined on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
with uniform distribution.

Assumption 2 assumes that
\sum\nolimits_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}\leq a_{-1}<\infty
, hence
\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mathscr{K}(x,x)=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Phi(\left\langle x,x\right\rangle)\leq\sum\nolimits_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}\leq a_{-1}
. Therefore, the integral operator defined in (3) is bounded:

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}=\text{Tr}(T):=\int\mathscr{K}(x,x)~\mathsf{d}\rho_{\mathcal{X}}(x)\leq a_{-1}.

Moreover, since Proposition 2.1 implies (
97
), we know that Assumption 8 holds for inner product kernels.

Random feature kernels defined on the hypercube
\{-1,1\}^{d}
with uniform distribution.

From Appendix D.2 and E.2 in
54
, eigen-decomposition for the random feature kernels defined on the hypercube is similar to inner-product kernels on the sphere. Especially, the addition formula holds for both of them (see, e.g., Appendix E.2.2 in
54
).
This means that we can write
\mathscr{K}(x,x^{\prime})=\Phi(\left\langle x,x^{\prime}\right\rangle)
, since

\mathscr{K}(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mu_{k}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{N}(d,k)}Y_{k,j}(x)Y_{k,j}(x^{\prime})\overset{\text{Addition formula}}{=}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mu_{k}\tilde{N}(d,k)Q_{k}^{(d)}(<x,x^{\prime}>),

where
\tilde{N}(d,k)
is the multiplicity of
\mu_{k}
defined in
54
, and
Q_{k}^{(d)}(\cdot)
is the
k
-th degree hyper-cubic Gegenbauer defined as Appendix E.2.2 in
54
. Therefore, if we assume Assumption 2 again,
then the proof for the random feature kernels defined on the hypercube follows from the same proof as for the inner-product kernels on the sphere, and thus Assumption 8 holds for random feature kernels.

Gaussian kernels defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with standard Gaussian distribution.

Notice that from the definition of Gaussian kernels, we have
\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mathscr{K}(x,x)\leq\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}
. Hence

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}=\text{Tr}(T):=\int\mathscr{K}(x,x)~\mathsf{d}\rho_{\mathcal{X}}(x)\leq\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}.

Moreover, from Section 4.3 in
64
, we know that the eigenspaces of Gaussian kernels defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with standard Gaussian distribution have
\Theta(d^{k})
eigenvalues of order
\Theta(d^{-k})
.
Therefore, Assumption 8 holds for Gaussian kernels.

Product Laguerre kernels defined in (
96
).

The following lemma shows that
\tilde{\mathscr{K}}_{\alpha,r}
defined in (
96
) is a kernel satisfies Assumption 8.

Lemma I.4
.

Let
\tilde{\mathscr{K}}_{\alpha,r}
be defined as (
96
). Then it is a continuous and positive-definite kernel on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
with respect to
\rho_{\mathcal{X}}
, where
\mathcal{X}=[0,\infty)^{d}
, and
\rho_{\mathcal{X}}
is the product measure with each coordinate
x_{(i)}
following an independent Gamma distribution
\Gamma(\alpha_{i}+1,1)
. Moreover, it admits a Mercer decomposition given in (22), with eigenvalues and eigenfunctions satisfying Assumption 8 with
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}=4^{r}
.

Proof.

It is obvious that
\tilde{\mathscr{K}}_{\alpha,r}
is a continuous and positive-definite kernel.
We then show that

\tilde{\mathscr{K}}_{\alpha,r}(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}\tilde{\psi}_{k,j}(x)\tilde{\psi}_{k,j}\left(x^{\prime}\right),

and provide explicit expressions of
\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}
’s and
\tilde{\psi}_{k,j}
’s.
For any
c>0
, let
\{L_{k}^{(c)}(\cdot)\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
be the generalized Laguerre polynomials (see, e.g., Chapter 6.2 in
2
)
and
\tilde{L}_{k}^{(c)}:=\sqrt{k!\Gamma(c+1)/\Gamma(k+c+1)}L_{k}^{c}(\cdot)
,
k\geq 0
, then we have (see, e.g., Eq. (6.2.3) in
2
):

\displaystyle~\int_{0}^{\infty}\tilde{L}_{k}^{(c)}(x)\tilde{L}_{m}^{(c)}(x)\frac{1}{\Gamma(c+1)}x^{c}e^{-x}~\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sqrt{\frac{k!m!}{\Gamma(k+c+1)\Gamma(m+c+1)}}\int_{0}^{\infty}L_{k}^{(c)}(x)L_{m}^{(c)}(x)x^{c}e^{-x}~\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sqrt{\frac{k!m!}{\Gamma(k+c+1)\Gamma(m+c+1)}}\frac{\Gamma(k+c+1)}{k!}\mathbf{1}\{k=m\}=\mathbf{1}\{k=m\}.

Furthermore, it is known that the generalized Laguerre polynomials satisfy the Hardy–Hille formula (see, e.g., Eq. (2.13) in
19
), that is,

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}_{i}(z,z^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\Gamma(\alpha_{i}+1)\cdot\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{k!(r/d)^{k}}{\Gamma(k+\alpha_{i}+1)}L_{k}^{(\alpha_{i})}(z)L_{k}^{(\alpha_{i})}(z^{\prime})

(98)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(r/d)^{k}\tilde{L}_{k}^{(\alpha_{i})}(z)\tilde{L}_{k}^{(\alpha_{i})}(z^{\prime}).

Combining (
96
) and (
98
), we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathscr{K}}_{\alpha,r}(x,x^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}\cdots\sum_{k_{d}=0}^{\infty}(r/d)^{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{d}}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\tilde{L}_{k_{i}}^{(\alpha_{i})}(x_{(i)})\tilde{L}_{k_{i}}^{(\alpha_{i})}(x_{(i)}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(r/d)^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\cdots,k_{d}\geq 0\\
\sum_{i\leq d}k_{i}=k\end{subarray}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{d}\tilde{L}_{k_{i}}^{(\alpha_{i})}(x_{(i)})\right]\left[\prod_{i=1}^{d}\tilde{L}_{k_{i}}^{(\alpha_{i})}(x_{(i)}^{\prime})\right],

implying that
\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}=(r/d)^{k}
and
\tilde{N}(d,k)=C_{k+d-1}^{d-1}
. Therefore, we have

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{N}(d,k)}\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(r/d)^{k}C_{k+d-1}^{d-1}\overset{\text{Generating function identity}}{=}(1-r/d)^{-(d-1+1)}\overset{d\geq 2r}{\leq}4^{r};

Moreover, for any
k=0,1,\cdots,2\ell_{\gamma}+2
, we have

\tilde{\lambda}_{k,j}=(r/d)^{k}=\Theta_{d}(d^{-k}),\quad j\leq\tilde{N}(d,k)=C_{k+d-1}^{d-1}=\Theta_{d}(d^{k}).

\blacksquare

I.5
Assumption 9 may hold even when the assumption in
57
fails

We present an example that Assumption 9 holds while the assumption in
57
does not hold.

Consider
\mathcal{X}=[0,1]
. We have that
\{e_{j}(x):=e^{2\pi ijx}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}([0,1])
, and that

\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\|e_{j}\|_{L^{q}}=\left(\int_{0}^{1}|e_{j}(x)|^{q}~\mathsf{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}=1,\quad q\geq 2,

hence Assumption 9 holds.

On the contrary, define
N=\lfloor(4C)^{2(\ell_{\gamma}+1)}\pi^{4}/8\rfloor+1
and
f_{\star}(x):=N^{-1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}e_{j}(x)
. Then
\left\|f_{\star}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}={\sum_{j=1}^{N}N^{-1}}=1
. However, for
q=4
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f_{\star}\right\|_{L^{4}}^{4}=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{N^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\left|\frac{e^{2\pi i(N+1)x}-e^{2\pi ix}}{e^{2\pi ix}-1}\right|^{4}~\mathsf{d}x=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\left|\frac{e^{\pi i(N+2)x}(e^{\pi iNx}-e^{-\pi iNx})}{e^{\pi ix}(e^{\pi ix}-e^{-\pi ix})}\right|^{4}~\mathsf{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{N^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\left|e^{\pi i(N+1)x}\right|^{4}\left|\frac{\sin(\pi N x)}{\sin(\pi x)}\right|^{4}~\mathsf{d}x=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\left|\frac{\sin(\pi N x)}{\sin(\pi x)}\right|^{4}~\mathsf{d}x

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{N^{2}}\int_{0}^{(2N)^{-1}}\left|\frac{\sin(\pi N x)}{\sin(\pi x)}\right|^{4}~\mathsf{d}x

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(A)}}{\geq}

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{N^{2}}\int_{0}^{(2N)^{-1}}\left|\frac{\frac{2}{\pi}\pi Nx}{\pi x}\right|^{4}~\mathsf{d}x=\frac{8}{\pi^{4}}N>\left[(Cq)^{(\ell_{\gamma}+1)/2}\left\|f_{\star}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right]^{4},

where the inequality (A) holds since for any
\theta\in[0,\pi/2]
, we have
2\theta/\pi\leq\sin(\theta)\leq\theta
.
This implies that the assumption in
57
does not hold.

Appendix J
Properties of
\varphi_{\lambda}

J.1
Additional requirements on
\varphi_{\lambda}

We list two additional requirements on
\varphi_{\lambda}
that are borrowed from
49
.

•

If
\tau<\infty
, then there exist positive constants
\mathfrak{C}_{7}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{8}
only depending on
\tau
, such that we have

\displaystyle(z/\lambda)^{2\tau}\psi_{\lambda}^{2}(z)\geq\mathfrak{C}_{7},\quad

\displaystyle\forall\lambda\in(0,1),z>\lambda

\displaystyle(z/\lambda)^{2\tau}\psi_{\lambda}^{2}(z)\leq\mathfrak{C}_{8}z\varphi_{\lambda}(z),\quad

\displaystyle\forall\lambda\in(0,1),z\leq\lambda.

•

Let

\displaystyle D_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\real z\in[-\lambda/2,\kappa^{2}],~\absolutevalue{\Im z}\leq\real z+\lambda/2\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\absolutevalue{z - \kappa^2}\leq\kappa^{2}+\lambda/2,~\real z\geq\kappa^{2}\right\};

Then
\varphi_{\lambda}(z)
can be extended to be an analytic function on some domain containing
D_{\lambda}

and the following conditions hold for all
\lambda\in(0,1)
:

(C1)

\left|(z+\lambda)\varphi_{\lambda}(z)\right|\leq\tilde{E}
for all
z\in D_{\lambda}
;

(C2)

\left|(z+\lambda)\psi_{\lambda}(z)\right|\leq\tilde{F}\lambda
for all
z\in D_{\lambda}
;

where
\tilde{E},\tilde{F}
are positive constants.

J.2
An identity of
\varphi_{\lambda}
as a matrix function

The following identity will be used in the proof of our main theorem.

Proposition J.1
.

Suppose
A,B
are semi-positive definite matrices,
A+B
is a positive definite matrix. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}-A\varphi_{\lambda}(A+B)=

\displaystyle~A(A+B)^{-1}\psi_{\lambda}(A+B)+B(A+B)^{-1}.

Proof.

Denote
M:=A+B
. Recall that
\psi_{\lambda}(M)=\mathrm{I}-M\varphi_{\lambda}(M)
. Then

\displaystyle\quad~AM^{-1}\psi_{\lambda}(M)+BM^{-1}

\displaystyle=AM^{-1}\big(\mathrm{I}-M\varphi_{\lambda}(M)\big)+BM^{-1}

\displaystyle=AM^{-1}-AM^{-1}M\varphi_{\lambda}(M)+BM^{-1}

\displaystyle=(A+B)M^{-1}-A\varphi_{\lambda}(M)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{I}-A\varphi_{\lambda}(M).

\blacksquare

J.3
Verification of Assumption 5 for Common Spectral Algorithms

In this subsection, we demonstrate that several commonly used spectral algorithms, as listed in Section 2.3.1, satisfy Assumption 5.

Given
0\leq u<\gamma<1
, we have
n^{-1}=o_{d}(\lambda)
. By Proposition
G.1
, there exist constants
\mathfrak{C}_{5},\mathfrak{C}_{6}>0
, such that we have
\mathfrak{C}_{5}\mathrm{I}_{n}\leq K_{>0}\leq\mathfrak{C}_{6}\mathrm{I}_{n}
. Recalling the decomposition that
K=K_{\leq 0}+K_{>0}=K_{0}+K_{>0}
, we now verify the assumption for each algorithm.

•

KRR.
We have

\displaystyle\psi_{\lambda}(K/n)=\lambda(K_{0}/n+K_{>0}/n+\lambda\mathrm{I}_{n})^{-1}=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)\lambda(K_{0}/n+\lambda\mathrm{I}_{n})^{-1}=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)\psi_{\lambda}(K_{0}/n).

•

Kernel gradient flow.
We have

\displaystyle\psi_{\lambda}(K/n)=e^{-(K_{0}/n+K_{>0}/n)/\lambda}=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)\psi_{\lambda}(K_{0}/n).

•

Iterated ridge regression.
We have

\displaystyle\psi_{\lambda}(K/n)=\lambda^{q}(K_{0}/n+K_{>0}/n+\lambda\mathrm{I}_{n})^{-q}=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)\lambda^{q}(K_{0}/n+\lambda\mathrm{I}_{n})^{-q}=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)\psi_{\lambda}(K_{0}/n).

•

Kernel gradient descent.
Denote
\Delta=-\eta K_{>0}/n
,
S_{0}=\mathrm{I}_{n}-\eta K_{0}/n
, and
S_{1}=S_{0}+\Delta
. Therefore, when
\eta
is sufficiently small (depending on
\mathfrak{C}_{5},\mathfrak{C}_{6}
), we have
\lambda_{\min}(S_{0})=\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)
and
\Delta=o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)
, hence from Taylor’s expansion, we have

\displaystyle\ln(S_1)\geq\ln(S_0)+\int_{0}^{1}(S_{0}+t\Delta)^{-1}\Delta(S_{0}+t\Delta)^{-1}~\mathrm{d}t-O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(\|\Delta\|_{2}^{2})\mathrm{I}_{n}.

Recall that
\psi_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{GD}}(z)=(1-\eta z)^{1/(\eta\lambda)}

and notice that
\frac{1}{\eta\lambda}\|\Delta\|_{2}^{2}=\eta\cdot(n^{2}\lambda)^{-1}\|K_{>0}\|_{2}^{2}\to 0
in probability, since we have
n^{2}\lambda=d^{2\gamma-u}\to\infty
by the assumption that
u<\gamma
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\psi_{\lambda}(K/n)=

\displaystyle e^{\frac{1}{\eta\lambda}\ln(S_1)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)e^{\frac{1}{\eta\lambda}\ln(S_0)}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{n\lambda}\int_{0}^{1}(S_{0}+t\Delta)^{-1}K_{>0}(S_{0}+t\Delta)^{-1}~\mathrm{d}t\right\}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\Omega_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)e^{\frac{1}{\eta\lambda}\ln(S_0)}.

Appendix K
Auxiliary Lemmas

The following proposition is known as Weyl’s inequality.

Proposition K.1

(Weyl’s inequality)
.

Let
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
be symmetric matrices on
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, with their eigenvalues ordered in descending order. Then, for any indices
i
and
j
, we have:

\lambda_{i+j-1}(A_{1}+A_{2})\leq\lambda_{i}(A_{1})+\lambda_{j}(A_{2})\leq\lambda_{i+j-n}(A_{1}+A_{2}).

The following proposition is a restatement of von Neumann’s trace inequality; see also
52
.

Proposition K.2
.

For Hermitian
n\times n
positive semi-definite complex matrices
A
and
B
, where now the eigenvalues are sorted decreasingly (
a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\cdots\geq a_{n}\geq 0
and
b_{1}\geq b_{2}\geq\cdots\geq b_{n}\geq 0
, respectively), we have

\mathrm{tr}\left(AB\right)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}b_{n-i+1}.

The following proposition is an extension of the symmetric matrix Bernstein inequality.

Proposition K.3

(Restate Exercise 6.10 in
68
)
.

Let
\{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be a collection of
n
independent, zero-mean matrices in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
, such that
\|A_{i}\|_{2}\leq b
almost surely.
Let

\sigma^{2}:=\max\left\{\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[A_{i}A_{i}^{\top}]\right\|_{2},\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[A_{i}^{\top}A_{i}]\right\|_{2}\right\}.

Then, for any
\delta>0
, the following tail bound holds:

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\right\|_{2}\geq\delta\right)\leq 2(d_{1}+d_{2})\exp\left(-\frac{\delta^{2}}{2(n\sigma^{2}+b\delta)}\right).

The following proposition restates the Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula.

Proposition K.4

(Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula)
.

Suppose
k<n
,
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is an invertible matrix, and
Z\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
is such that
ZZ^{\top}+A
is invertible. Then we have

(ZZ^{\top}+A)^{-1}=A^{-1}-A^{-1}Z(\mathrm{I}_{k}+Z^{\top}A^{-1}Z)^{-1}Z^{\top}A^{-1}.

Appendix L
Further Experiments and Supporting Proofs

L.1
Additional Experiments

Figures
7
and
8
report results of the remaining Type 1 experiments with optimal
C_{\lambda}
value.

Figure
9
,
10
,
11
, and
12
report results of all Type 1 experiments with different
C_{\lambda}
values.

L.2
Verification of the regression function in (23)

In this subsection, we show that
f_{\star}
defined as (23) satisfies Assumption 3 and Assumption 6. Without loss of generality, we assume that
d\geq\mathfrak{C}
such that results in Proposition 2.1 hold.

In the following proof, we will frequently use the addition formula (see, e.g., Proposition 1.18 in
28
, page 56 in
31
, or (39) in
48
):

P_{k,d}(\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle)=\frac{1}{N(d,k)}\sum_{j=1}^{N(d,k)}\psi_{k,j}(x)\psi_{k,j}(x^{\prime}),\quad k=0,1,\cdots

(99)

where
P_{k,d}(\cdot)
is the
k
-th normalized Gegenbauer polynomial (such that
P_{k,d}(1)=1
) with parameter
(d-2)/2
, and we have

\displaystyle P_{0,d}(t)=1,\quad P_{1,d}(t)=t,\quad P_{2,d}(t)=\frac{dt^{2}-1}{d-1}.

(100)

Case
s=1
.
In this case we have

\displaystyle f_{\star}(x)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{3}\mathscr{K}(\xi_{i},x)\overset{(4)}{=}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{3}\lambda_{j}\phi_{j}(\xi_{i})\right]\phi_{j}(x)

\displaystyle(\because\text{Proposition 2.1})\quad=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\ell_{\gamma}+1}\sum_{j=1}^{N(d,k)}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{3}\mu_{k}\psi_{k,j}(\xi_{i})\right]\psi_{k,j}(x)+\sum_{j=N_{\ell_{\gamma}+1}+1}^{\infty}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{3}\lambda_{j}\phi_{j}(\xi_{i})\right]\phi_{j}(x).

We first note that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{[\mathcal{H}]}^{2}=

\displaystyle~\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}^{-1}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{3}\lambda_{j}\phi_{j}(\xi_{i})\right]^{2}\leq 3\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}\phi_{j}(\xi_{i})\phi_{j}(\xi_{i})=3\sum_{i=1}^{3}\mathscr{K}(\xi_{i},\xi_{i})\leq 9K_{\max},

thus
f_{\star}
satisfies Assumption 3.

Since we have set
\gamma<2
and
d>10
in our experiments, we have
\ell_{\gamma}\leq 1
and
3-6/(d-1)>2
. Therefore, for any
m\leq\ell_{\gamma}+1\leq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\mu_{m}^{-1}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N(d,m)}\theta_{m,j}^{2}

(101)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{m}^{-1}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N(d,m)}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{3}\mu_{m}\psi_{m,j}(\xi_{i})\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{m}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N(d,m)}\psi_{m,j}^{2}(\xi_{i})+\sum_{i\neq i^{\prime}}^{3}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N(d,m)}\psi_{m,j}(\xi_{i})\psi_{m,j}(\xi_{i^{\prime}})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eqn_addition_formula})}{=}

\displaystyle\mu_{m}N(d,m)\left[\sum_{i=1}^{3}P_{k,d}(\langle\xi_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle)+\sum_{i\neq i^{\prime}}^{3}P_{k,d}(\langle\xi_{i},\xi_{i^{\prime}}\rangle)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eqn_former_gegenbauer})}{=}

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{matrix}\mu_{0}N(d,0)[3+6]=9\mu_{0}N(d,0),&m=0;\\
\mu_{1}N(d,1)[3+\sum_{i\neq i^{\prime}}^{3}\langle\xi_{i},\xi_{i^{\prime}}\rangle]\overset{(24)}{\geq}0.1\mu_{1}N(d,1),&m=1;\\
\mu_{2}N(d,2)[3+\sum_{i\neq i^{\prime}}^{3}\frac{d\langle\xi_{i},\xi_{i^{\prime}}\rangle^{2}-1}{d-1}]\geq\mu_{2}N(d,2)[3-6/(d-1)]>2\mu_{2}N(d,2),&m=2;\\
\end{matrix}\right.

Therefore, from Proposition 2.1 we have
\mu_{m}^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{N(d,m)}\theta_{m,j}^{2}=\Omega_{d}(1)
,
m=0,1,2
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\tilde{\ell}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N(d,k)}\theta_{k,j}^{2}\geq

\displaystyle~\theta_{0,1}^{2}=\left[\sum_{i=1}^{3}\mu_{0}\psi_{0,1}(\xi_{i})\right]^{2}=9\mu_{0}^{2}=\Theta_{d}(1),

where the last equation follows from Proposition 2.1.
We conclude that
f_{\star}
satisfies Assumption 6.

Case
s\neq 1
and
\mathscr{K}=\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{rbf}}
.
In this case we have

\displaystyle f_{\star}(x)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sum_{k=0}^{2}d^{k(1-s)/2}P_{k,d}(\langle\xi_{i},x\rangle)\overset{(\ref{eqn_addition_formula})}{=}\sum_{k=0}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N(d,k)}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{d^{k(1-s)/2}}{N(d,k)}\psi_{k,j}(\xi_{i})\right]\psi_{k,j}(x)

By Proposition 2.1, we have

\displaystyle\|f\|_{[\mathcal{H}]^{s}}^{2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N(d,k)}\mu_{k}^{-s}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{d^{k(1-s)/2}}{N(d,k)}\psi_{k,j}(\xi_{i})\right]^{2}\overset{(\ref{eqn_addition_formula})}{\leq}3\sum_{k=0}^{2}\frac{\mu_{k}^{-s}d^{k(1-s)}}{N(d,k)}=O_{d}(1),

which implies that
f_{\star}
satisfies Assumption 3.

Since we have set
\gamma<2
and
d>10
in our experiments, we have
\ell_{\gamma}\leq 1
and
3-6/(d-1)>2
.
Therefore, for any
m\leq\ell_{\gamma}+1\leq 2
, similar to (
101
), we have

\displaystyle\mu_{m}^{-s}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N(d,m)}\theta_{m,j}^{2}=

\displaystyle\mu_{m}^{-s}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N(d,m)}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{d^{m(1-s)/2}}{N(d,m)}\psi_{m,j}(\xi_{i})\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{m}^{-s}d^{m(1-s)}}{N(d,m)^{2}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N(d,m)}\psi_{m,j}^{2}(\xi_{i})+\sum_{i\neq i^{\prime}}^{3}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N(d,m)}\psi_{m,j}(\xi_{i})\psi_{m,j}(\xi_{i^{\prime}})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Omega_{d}\left(\frac{\mu_{m}^{-s}d^{m(1-s)}}{N(d,m)}\right).

By Proposition 2.1, the right-hand side of the last display is
\Omega_{d}(1)
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\tilde{\ell}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N(d,k)}\theta_{k,j}^{2}\geq

\displaystyle\theta_{0,1}^{2}=\left[\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{d^{0(1-s)/2}}{N(d,0)}\psi_{0,1}(\xi_{i})\right]^{2}=\frac{9}{N(d,0)^{2}}=\Theta_{d}(1),

where the last equation follows from Proposition 2.1.
Therefore,
f_{\star}
satisfies Assumption 6.

Case
s\neq 1
and
\mathscr{K}=\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{ntk}}
.

In our NTK experiments, we always set
\ell_{\gamma}\leq 1
. Moreover, for the NTK kernel we have
a_{j}>0
for
j\in\{0,1\}\cup\{2,4,6,\cdots\}
. Therefore, the argument follows the same steps as in the RBF kernel case.
